of the narrator’s gender and its impact on the text is a central one in the field of feminist narratology, which explores the ways in which sex, gender, and sexuality shape both narrative forms and meanings. The narrator’s gender, especially when it is that of a girl child, may have significant implications for the representation of the self and the world, the construction of the plot and the point of view, the expression of the voice and the style, and the reception and interpretation of the text by different audiences. To recognize this specificity, as Susan Lanser argues, is to challenge the assumption that narrative is a gender-neutral or gender-indifferent mode of discourse, and to acknowledge that gender is a category “that is relevant to the analysis of the structural features of narratives.” In order to understand the true “nature, form, and functioning of narrative” (Lanser), it is imperative to explore the implications of “sex, gender and/or sexuality” (Lanser) of the narrator or narrative. She claims that texts with, I would say girl child narrators, are more “polyphones”; they comprise different strands which need to be “identified” and “disentangled,” being multi-layered, and with different levels of meaning. (Toward A feminist Narratology Jstor p 349-50) While “individuals may differ on the extent to which biological sex constructs identity,” there is a “broad consensus that gender infuses our material and conceptual realities in myriad tangible and intangible ways.” Even Eve Sedgwick, Nancy Miller, among others, have looked into the ways gender colours the production of story in narrative. So drawing from what Lanser and others suggest it is not about diminishing the girl child narrator’s account, but rather, it calls for a deeper analysis of the narrative itself within the social context it’s situated. Is the narrative interacting with the underlying currents of gender biases, and if so, how does this interaction shape the story being told?.Generally is examined what is told, but not how it is told. To study the narrator’s testimonial and interpretative functions, it becomes necessary to look at the narrator’s utterances within a context. Nothing prohibits us, as Warhol does, from asking, among other questions, what part of the narrator’s gender plays in the kinds of interventions he or she uses in narrative. Such an inquiry can reveal the essential differences between the sexes - between their psychological natures, or between their operation as subjects or signifiers in history and philosophy 4. It can also uncover the diversity and complexity of gendered experiences and expressions that resist binary or essentialist categorizations. \n\n", "chosen": "The issue of the narrator's gender and its influence on the text is a crucial topic in the field of feminist narratology. This area of study investigates how sex, gender, and sexuality shape both narrative forms and meanings. Susan Lanser argues that recognizing this specificity is essential to challenging the assumption that narrative is a gender-neutral or gender-indifferent mode of discourse. While \"individuals may differ on the extent to which biological sex constructs identity,\" there is a \"broad consensus that gender infuses our material and conceptual realities in myriad tangible and intangible ways\" (Sedgwick et al.). Feminist narratologists, such as Eve Sedgwick and Nancy Miller, have also examined the ways gender influences the production of story in narrative.\n\nTherefore, it is not a matter of diminishing the girl child narrator's account, but rather, it calls for a deeper analysis of the narrative itself within the social context it's situated.
